This page focuses on tobacco availability, use, treatment and harms in Australia, including both legal tobacco products and illicit tobacco. The reporting uses data from a range of sources, mostly national administrative and survey data. For related content on the use of vapes and other nicotine products, see Vapes and other nicotine products.

#### Key findings

- Daily tobacco smoking has declined substantially since the early 2000s, and more people are choosing to never take up smoking
- Tobacco smoking has declined among both men and women, but men continue to be more likely than women to smoke daily
- Over 2 in 3 people who smoked in 2025 had future intentions to quit smoking or had already given up
- Tobacco use is the second leading risk factor for the burden of disease in Australia, contributing to 7.6% of disease burden in 2024

### What data sources are available?

#### Data sources for tobacco availability, use, harms and treatment

- Alcohol and Other Drug Treatment Services National Minimum Data Set
- Australian Burden of Disease Study
- Australian Taxation Office
- Household, Income and Labour Dynamics in Australia survey
- National Drug Strategy Household Survey
- National Health Survey
- National Perinatal Data Collection
- Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme data collection
- Tobacco in Australia: Facts and Issues

### What do we know about tobacco availability in Australia?

There are a range of data sources that contain information about tobacco use, harms and treatment. These include self-report surveys that ask people about their use of tobacco, health administrative data sets (such as administrative data routinely collected by hospitals), and burden of disease analysis. Each data set uses a different methodology, and the language used to describe tobacco use may also differ across sources.

For more information about each data source, see Technical notes.

### 1 in 3

- Updated people (34%) who currently smoke had recently used illicit tobacco in 2025, up from 17% in 2022–2023. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

#### Consumer and household spending on tobacco products

Analysis of tobacco sales, customs and excise data indicate that there has been an overall decline in estimated consumer spending on tobacco in Australia over the past decade:

However, these data do not account for spending on illicit tobacco products where customs and excise duty may not have been paid (Bayly and Scollo 2025).

Information on average spending on tobacco can also be obtained from surveys of Australian households, such as the Household, Income, and Labour Dynamics (HILDA) survey. Analysis of HILDA data indicates that there was a 2.6% decline in the average amount of reported tobacco expenditure across all Australian households (not just those with people who smoke) between 2006 and 2022 (Bayly and Scollo 2025). However, this decline may reflect fewer households making tobacco purchases over time:

For more information on illicit tobacco use and markets in Australia, see Tobacco in Australia: Facts and issues.

- Analysis of customs and excise data indicates that the estimated amount that Australians spent on tobacco declined from $37 billion to $15 billion between 2014 and 2024 (expressed in 2024 prices, adjusting for seasonality and increasing prices of tobacco products) (Bayly and Scollo 2025).
- Analysis of data from tobacco companies, which is routinely provided to market research companies and compiled by international research agencies, has shown that the number of manufactured cigarettes sold in Australia declined from 15.7 billion sticks in 2016 to 5.0 billion in 2024 (Scollo and Bayly 2025). This represents a decline from 800 to 223 cigarettes per capita, noting that stock shipments in the year to December 2024 would be artificially low due to the introduction of new restrictions on packaging, product names and contents from 1 April 2025.

- Among households with any tobacco expenditure, the average amount spent on tobacco increased by 28% between 2006 and 2022 after adjusting for inflation (from $3,840 to $4,932, expressed in 2022 prices)
- Over the same period, the proportion of households with any tobacco expenditure fell from 26% to 20% (Bayly and Scollo, 2025).

#### Illicit tobacco markets

Data on the availability of illicit tobacco in Australia have previously been limited. The Australian Government established the role of Illicit Tobacco and E-cigarette (ITEC) Commissioner in July 2024 to support the development of national strategies addressing illicit tobacco and e-cigarettes. The first ITEC report was released in 2025, providing insights into Australia’s illicit tobacco and e-cigarette market in 2024–25 (ITEC 2025).

Estimates of the size of the illicit tobacco market in industry-funded reports have historically differed from estimates provided by ITEC, the Australian Taxation Office (ATO) and the National Drug Strategy Household Survey (NDSHS). However, most data sources indicate that illicit tobacco use is rising and illicit tobacco products now account for a substantial portion of the overall tobacco market in Australia:

For more information on illicit tobacco use and markets in Australia, see Tobacco in Australia: Facts and issues.

- Self-report survey data from the NDSHS indicate that around 1 in 3 (34%) people who currently smoke had recently used illicit tobacco in 2025. This represents a substantial increase from 2022–2023 when 17% had done so. Among people who currently smoke:the proportion who had purchased 15 or more packets of tobacco products without plain packaging also increased, from 4% in 2022–2023 to 10% in 2025those who had recently used illicit tobacco smoked a higher number of cigarettes per day on average (12.8) than those who had not (9.0) (AIHW 2026b, tables 1.30, 1.33 and 1.44).
- ITEC estimated that illicit tobacco accounted for 50–60% of the total tobacco market in 2024–25, with a value of $4.1–$6.9 billion. This estimate is based on modelling of 2022–2023 consumption data from the NDSHS (ITEC 2025).
- The ATO estimated that around 25% (1,741 tonnes) of all tobacco for sale in 2023–24 was illicit. The amount of lost customs and excise duty from illicit tobacco ($3.2 billion) accounted for an estimated net gap of 19.6% between the expected and actual customs and excise duty collected from tobacco products, up from 7.5% in 2018–19 (ATO 2025a). However, the ATO advises using caution for these data (ATO 2025b).
- Industry estimates, based on consumer surveys and empty pack surveys, indicated that illicit tobacco consumption accounted for around 29% of the total tobacco market in 2023, up from 11.8% in 2012 (Cho et al. 2025).

### What do we know about people who use tobacco?

#### How many people use tobacco and has it changed over time?

- Updated There have been long-term declines in tobacco smoking in Australia since 2001. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

The most recent data on tobacco smoking in Australia are from the 2025 NDSHS, which showed continued declines in daily smoking. Between 2001 and 2025, the proportion of people aged 14 and over who:

Data from the 2022 National Health Survey (NHS) showed a similar pattern to the NDSHS data over time. The proportion of adults who smoke daily declined steadily over the 2 decades to 2022, and, after adjusting for age, has halved from 22.4% in 2001 to 10.6% in 2022 (ABS 2023).

The long-term decline in daily smoking has largely been driven by people never taking up smoking, alongside the proportion of people quitting smoking also rising over time (AIHW 2026b, Greenhalgh et al. 2025).

- smoked daily declined from 19% in 2001 to 5.6% in 2025
- have never smoked has increased from around 1 in 2 (51%) in 2001 to around 2 in 3 (69%) in 2025 (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.1; Figure 2).

#### Does tobacco use differ by age and gender?

As well as people becoming less likely to smoke over time, those who do smoke are starting later in life and smoking fewer cigarettes than in the past:

- Among people aged 14 and over who have ever smoked a full cigarette, the average age at which they smoked their first full cigarette rose from 15.7 in 2001 to 16.8 in 2025 (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.17).
- People who currently smoke are smoking fewer cigarettes, declining from an average of 15.6 cigarettes per day in 2001 to 11.2 in 2025 (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.8).

- Updated Tobacco smoking has declined among both men and women, but men continue to be more likely than women to smoke daily. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Daily tobacco smoking is typically highest among males and people in older age groups (AIHW 2026b; Figure 3). Findings from the 2025 NDSHS showed that, between 2022–2023 and 2025, daily smoking rates:

Similarly, data from the 2022 NHS also showed that daily smoking rates were highest among males and those aged 45–64 (ABS 2023, Table 14.3).

- decreased for both males and females aged 14 and over, but males remained 1.2 times as likely as females to smoke daily (6.1% of males and 5.1% of females in 2025)
- decreased among most age groups, but remained highest among people aged in their 40s, 50s and 60s (6.7%, 7.9% and 8.3%, respectively, in 2025) (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.4; Figure 3).

#### What types of tobacco products do people use?

Most people in Australia who smoke use manufactured cigarettes, either on their own or in addition to roll-your-own cigarettes, but patterns of use have changed over time.

Data from the 2025 NDSHS found that, among people who currently smoke:

Following a ban on menthol in tobacco products, which came into full effect from 1 July 2025, the proportion of people who smoke and reported use of menthol cigarettes (manufactured or roll‑your‑own) in the previous 12 months declined from 31% in 2022–2023 to 23% in 2025 (AIHW 2026b, DHDA 2025).

- the proportion who exclusively smoked manufactured cigarettes declined from a peak of 74% in 2004 to 59% in 2025
- the proportion who exclusively smoked roll-your-own cigarettes fluctuated, increasing from 5.7% in 2001 to a peak of 16% in 2022–2023, before falling to 7.7% in 2025
- the proportion who smoked a combination of manufactured and roll-your-own cigarettes rose from 22% in 2001 to 33% in 2025 (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.23).

#### How many people use illicit tobacco?

### Almost 3 in 5

- Updated people (56%) aged 18–24 who currently smoked in 2025 had recently used illicit tobacco, higher than any other age group. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Use of illicit tobacco is becoming increasingly common. Data from the NDSHS showed that in 2025, around 1 in 3 (34%) people who currently smoke had recently used illicit tobacco, a substantial increase from 17% in 2022–2023 (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.30).

In 2025, among people who currently smoked, recent use of illicit tobacco was more common among:

In 2025, among people who had purchased branded illicit tobacco in the previous 3 months, the most common sources were tobacconists (57%), local convenience stores (14%) and people selling tobacco independently (\*9.3%) (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.37).

\* Estimate has a relative standard error between 25% and 50% and should be interpreted with caution.

- males (38%) than females (27%)
- people aged 18–24 (56%) than all other age groups (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.32).

#### How many women smoke during pregnancy?

Tobacco smoking during pregnancy is a preventable risk factor for pregnancy complications, and support to stop smoking is widely available through antenatal clinics.

Data from the National Perinatal Data Collection indicates that tobacco smoking during pregnancy has been declining over time in Australia. In 2023, 7.8% (or 21,450) of all mothers who gave birth reported smoking at any time during pregnancy, down from 13% in 2011 (AIHW 2025).

There have been notable improvements over time in smoking rates at any time during pregnancy for some population groups including:

- First Nations mothers (from 49% in 2011 to 38% in 2023)
- mothers aged under 20 (from 36% in 2011 to 29% in 2023) (AIHW 2025).

#### Does tobacco use differ by geographic area?

Since 2010, the proportion of people aged 14 and over who smoke tobacco daily has declined across all socioeconomic areas and most remoteness areas (AIHW 2026b). Detailed information on tobacco use by geographic area, including state and territory data, is available in Geographic areas.

For related content on tobacco use among specific population groups in this report, see Population groups.

### What are the harms associated with tobacco use?

#### Burden of disease and injury

- Tobacco use continues to be one of the leading risk factors contributing to the burden of disease in Australia. Source: Australian Burden of Disease Study

#### Harms from second-hand smoke

In 2024, tobacco was the second highest risk factor contributing to the burden of disease in Australia and was responsible for 7.6% of the total burden of disease and injury (AIHW 2024). The age-standardised rate of total attributable burden due to tobacco use was 12.7 Disability-Adjusted Life Years (DALY) per 1,000 population in 2024, a decrease from 21.6 DALY per 1,000 population in 2003.

Tobacco use contributed to a number of diseases and injuries including:

For related content on the burden of disease due to tobacco, alcohol and other drugs in this report, see Burden of disease and injuries related to alcohol and other drugs.

- 73% of the burden due to lung cancer
- 71% of the burden due to chronic obstructive pulmonary disorder
- 69% of the burden due to laryngeal cancer
- 47% of the burden due to lip and oral cavity cancer
- 46% of the burden due to oesophageal cancer (AIHW 2024, Table S8).

- Updated Over the past twenty years, there has been a decrease in the proportion of households with children aged 14 and under that are exposed to tobacco smoke in the home. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

The inhalation of other people’s tobacco smoke can be harmful to health. Second-hand smoke can cause coronary heart disease and lung cancer in non-smoking adults and is known to induce and exacerbate a range of mild to severe respiratory effects in infants, children and adults. Second-hand smoke is also a cause of sudden infant death syndrome (SIDS) and a range of other serious health outcomes in young children. There is increasing evidence that second-hand smoke exposure is associated with psychological distress (Campbell et al. 2017).

Results from the 2025 NDSHS showed that fewer people are exposed to tobacco smoke at home. In 2025:

- 1.9% of households with children aged 14 and under had someone who smoked daily inside the house. This has remained stable since 2019 (2.1%), but is much lower than in 2001 (20%)
- 2.0% of adults who did not smoke were exposed to tobacco inside the home on a daily basis, down from 2.6% in 2022–2023 and 11% in 2001 (AIHW 2026b, tables 1.14 and 1.16).

#### Harms related to smoking during pregnancy

Compared with babies born to mothers who did not smoke at any time during pregnancy, babies born to mothers who smoked tobacco at any time during pregnancy were more likely to:

Results for 2023 were based on data from the National Perinatal Data Collection, while 2022 results were based on data from the National Perinatal Mortality Data Collection.

- be low birth weight, 13.7% compared with 5.9%, in 2023 (among liveborn babies)
- be born pre-term, 13.7% compared with 7.8%, in 2023 (among all babies)
- be admitted to a special care nursery or neonatal intensive care unit, 27.3% compared with 17.3%, in 2023 (among liveborn babies)
- have a higher perinatal mortality rate, 17.5 per 1,000 births compared with 9.3 per 1,000, in 2022 (among all babies) (AIHW 2025).

### How many people quit smoking or receive treatment for tobacco use?

#### Changes in smoking behaviour

### Almost 3 in 4

- Updated people who smoked in 2025 had changed their smoking behaviour. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Data from the 2025 NDSHS showed that almost 3 in 4 (72%) people who currently smoked had taken some action to change their smoking behaviour, including making efforts to reduce or give up their smoking (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.46).

Of those who had changed their smoking behaviour:

Successful smoking cessation may take many attempts over several years. Data from the HILDA survey indicate that between 2.1% and 3.5% of people quit smoking in any given year between 2003 and 2021. However, 3 in 5 (61.5%) people who quit smoking between 2003 and 2018 started again within 3 years (Wilkins et al. 2024). These data are likely an underestimate as people who quit and start smoking again between annual survey waves will not be counted in these estimates (Wilkins et al. 2024).

- 48% did so because it was costing too much, down from 53% in 2022–2023
- 45% did so because they thought it was affecting their health or fitness, the same as in 2022–2023 and 2019 (AIHW 2026b, Table 1.47).

#### Smoking cessation medicines

Data from Australia’s Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme (PBS) provide information about prescription medicines that are used to help people stop their smoking (smoking cessation medicines). Data from the PBS show that rates of dispensing for smoking cessation medicines fluctuated between 2012–13 and 2024–25, potentially influenced by factors including shortages of Varenicline in 2021–22 (tables PBS62 and PBS64). For detailed information on prescription smoking cessation medicines in this report, see Smoking and alcohol cessation medicines.

#### Specialist treatment for nicotine use

#### Nicotine treatment provided in alcohol and other drug treatment services

When considering nicotine treatment in Australia, consultation between a client and a service provider/clinician is an important step in determining a client’s treatment plan. Clients may actively seek treatment for nicotine dependence or be offered treatment for nicotine dependence by the service provider/clinician as a parallel treatment with other drugs of concern. Nicotine may be reported as a principal or additional drug of concern. Following consultation, a service provider/clinician will determine a treatment plan with the client.

Clients do not regularly seek (or are referred to) AOD treatment services for nicotine addiction treatment. Clients are more often referred to other avenues of treatment. This is due to the prevalence of other resources for the management of nicotine dependence and smoking cessation support, including helplines and access to nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) via general practitioners.

For more information, see Alcohol and other drug treatment services in Australia.

- Around 1,400 people received specialist treatment for nicotine as their principal drug of concern in 2024–25. Source: Alcohol and other drug treatment services in Australia report

### Where do I go for more information?

Nicotine (including both tobacco and e-cigarette use) accounts for a small proportion of specialist alcohol and other drug treatment episodes provided to clients each year (AIHW 2026). Nicotine was the principal drug of concern in just 1.0% (around 2,100) of treatment episodes in 2024–25, relatively stable since 2016–17 (1.3% or 2,584 episodes) (AIHW 2026, Table Drg.1).

Of the approximately 1,400 clients who received treatment for nicotine as their principal drug of concern in 2024–25:

For related content on nicotine treatment in this report, see Alcohol and other drug treatment services.

- over half (54%) were male
- 7 in 10 (71%) were aged under 40, including those aged 10–19 (36%), 20–29 (18%), or 30–39 (17%)
- around 1 in 4 (23%) were First Nations people (AIHW 2026, tables SC.9–SC.11).

- Illicit tobacco in the NDSHS
- Tobacco in Australia: Facts and issues
- Tobacco smoking in the NDSHS

## Alcohol, tobacco & other drugs in Australia, Vapes and other nicotine products

Source: https://www.aihw.gov.au/reports/alcohol/alcohol-tobacco-other-drugs-australia/contents/drug-types/vaping-and-e-cigarettes

# Alcohol, tobacco & other drugs in Australia

## Vapes and other nicotine products

Vapes and other nicotine products
is an
Australia's health
topic
Explore related topic summaries

- Tobacco 07 Oct 2026
- Alcohol 07 Oct 2026
- Burden of disease 12 Dec 2024

				In this section

Introduction
What data sources are available?
What do we know about the availability of vapes and other nicotine products in Australia?
What do we know about people who use vapes?
What do we know about people who use other nicotine products?
What are the harms associated with the use of vapes and other nicotine products?
Where do I go for more information?

### Introduction

#### What are vapes and other nicotine products?

Electronic cigarettes (‘e-cigarettes’), or vapes, are personal vaping devices designed to deliver nicotine and/or other chemicals via inhalation of an aerosol vapour (DHDA 2025). The solution used in vapes varies. Common e-liquids include propylene glycol, vegetable glycerol, and flavourings, and may contain nicotine in freebase or salt form (Banks et al. 2022).

From 1 July 2024, all vapes and vaping products (regardless of whether they contain nicotine or not) can only be sold in a pharmacy for the purpose of helping people quit smoking or manage nicotine dependence (DHDA 2025). People who require vapes with a nicotine concentration higher than 20mg/L and those under 18 years need a prescription to access vapes, subject to state and territory arrangements (DHDA 2024).

Other and emerging nicotine products are nicotine-containing products other than smoked tobacco products (such as cigarettes, cigars, pipe tobacco and bidis), e‑cigarettes, and nicotine-replacement therapies approved by the Therapeutic Goods Association (TGA) (AIHW 2026). There are several types of other nicotine products including chewing tobacco, water pipes with tobacco (including shishas, hookahs and nargilas), heated (heat-not-burn) tobacco products, snuff, traditional (tobacco containing) snus, oral nicotine pouches, and non-medical chewable or dissolvable nicotine products. The nicotine concentrations in these products are highly variable.

In this report, the other nicotine products described are:

The sale of snus and other smokeless tobacco products is banned in Australia, while nicotine pouches are subject to strict regulation. On 24 July 2026, new regulations commenced to restrict access to nicotine pouches. There is no lawful pathway for individuals to buy or import nicotine pouches into Australia unless the product is on the Australian Register of Therapeutic Goods or is included as part of a clinical trial (TGA 2026).

For more information on laws and policies related to vapes in this report, see Policy context.

- snus, which is a smokeless tobacco product placed between the upper lip and gum, allowing nicotine to be absorbed through the lining of the mouth
- oral nicotine pouches, which are similar to snus in appearance and use but typically do not contain tobacco. Instead, they contain nicotine along with flavourings and other ingredients (AIHW 2026).

The use of vapes ('vaping') has risen over the last decade and the use of certain other nicotine products, such as oral nicotine pouches, has also increased in recent years (AIHW 2026). Use of vapes and other nicotine products is more common among younger people than those in older age groups, and many people who use other nicotine products also use vapes (AIHW 2026).

The long-term harms of vapes and other nicotine products are not fully known, but people who use vapes are exposed to toxic chemicals that can cause adverse health effects (NHMRC 2022). Ongoing monitoring of vapes and other nicotine product use and harms is important to identify groups of people at increased risk of harm and potential areas for intervention.

This page focuses on the use of vapes and other nicotine products, as well as treatment and harms in Australia. The reporting uses data from a range of sources, mostly survey data. For related content on tobacco use in this report, see Tobacco.

#### Key findings

- The daily use of e-cigarettes remained similar between 2022–2023 and 2025 after previously rising, and is most common among people aged 18–24
- Recent use of other nicotine products is relatively less common, with use of oral nicotine pouches being most common among people aged 18–24
- The use of vapes and other nicotine products has been linked to numerous short- or medium-term health risks, but evidence for longer-term health effects is currently limited

### What data sources are available?

#### Data sources for e-cigarette use, treatment and harms

- Household, Income and Labour Dynamics in Australia Survey
- National Drug Strategy Household Survey
- National Health Survey

There are a limited number of data sources that contain information about e-cigarette and other nicotine product use, harms and treatment. These are mostly self-report surveys that ask people about their use of e-cigarettes or other nicotine products, with limited information available in health administrative data sets. Each data set uses a different methodology, and the language used to describe the use of vapes may also differ across sources. The terms ‘vapes’ and ‘e-cigarettes’ are used interchangeably on this page, according to the source report.

For more information on each data source, see Technical notes.

### What do we know about the availability of vapes and other nicotine products in Australia?

The 2025 National Drug Strategy Household Survey (NDSHS) was conducted after major reforms to e-cigarette regulations were put in place by the Australian Government in 2024. These regulations made it illegal to purchase e-cigarettes (whether they contained nicotine or not) from all retailers except for participating pharmacies, including retailers that sell tobacco products (TGA 2024). The results below reflect the prevalence of e-cigarette use in Australia after these restrictions came into effect.

Data from the 2025 NDSHS showed that most people who used e-cigarettes did not obtain them through pharmacies, despite pharmacies being the legal supply pathway in Australia. Only \*2.5% of people reported obtaining e-cigarettes from a physical pharmacy, and 4.8% from an online pharmacy. The remaining 93% obtained their e-cigarettes from other sources, including:

\* Estimate has a relative standard error of 25% to 50% and should be used with caution.

Information on the availability of other nicotine products in Australia is limited.

For related content on e-cigarette policy in this report, see Policy context.

- tobacco retail outlets or tobacconists (50%)
- friends or family members (17%)
- online retailers that were not pharmacies (8.6%)
- informal sellers or dealers not based at a retail outlet (8.2%) (AIHW 2026, Table 2.33).

### What do we know about people who use vapes?

#### How many people use vapes and has it changed over time?

### E-cigarette use among people aged 14+

- Updated 2016 1.2 2022–2023 7.0 2025 6.0 Current use of e-cigarettes declined between 2022–2023 and 2025, after previously rising. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey
- Updated Daily use of e-cigarettes remained stable between 2022–2023 and 2025, while occasional use fell. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

The 2025 NDSHS showed that e-cigarette use among people aged 14 and over increased substantially between 2016 and 2025 (AIHW 2026). The largest increase occurred between 2019 and 2022–2023, followed by stable daily use and a decline in current use in 2025. The decline in current use between 2022–2023 and 2025 was driven by reductions in weekly but not daily use (from 1.3% to 0.8%) and monthly but not weekly use (from 0.9% to 0.7%) (AIHW 2026, Table 2.1).

Between 2016 and 2025:

- daily use of e-cigarettes increased from 0.5% to 3.6%. Most of this increase was observed between 2019 (1.1%) and 2022–2023 (3.5%), before stabilising in 2025
- current use of e-cigarettes increased from 1.2% to 6.0%, with use peaking at 7.0% in 2022–2023 (AIHW 2026, tables 2.1 and 2.5; Figure 1).

#### Does the use of vapes differ by age and gender?

Previous data from the 2022 National Health Survey (NHS) showed that about 1 in 7 (14%) people aged 18 and over had used an e-cigarette or vaping device at least once, and 4.0% reported currently using a device (ABS 2023).

Among people who currently used e-cigarettes in 2025:

- 3 in 5 (60%) used them daily, up from 49% in 2022–2023
- around 6 in 7 (86%) reported the last e-cigarette they used contained nicotine, up from 73% in 2022–2023 (AIHW 2026, tables 2.4 and 2.10).

- Updated People aged 18–24 continue to have the highest rates of e-cigarette use, despite declining use among this age group. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Both current and daily use of e-cigarettes are generally more common among younger people than people in older age groups, despite recent declines in current use among younger age groups. Daily use was similar for males and females (AIHW 2026; Figure 1).

Data from the 2025 NDSHS showed that:

Data from the 2022 NHS also showed that men were more likely than women to currently use e-cigarettes or vaping devices (5.2% compared with 2.9%), while people aged 18–24 had the highest rates of current e-cigarette use (9.3%) (ABS 2023).

- people aged 18–24 continued to be the most likely to currently use e-cigarettes (14%), despite the rate among this age group declining from 21% in 2022–2023. Current e-cigarette use did not substantially change among other age groups
- people aged 18–24 also continued to have the highest rates of daily e-cigarette use (8.3%, stable from 9.3% in 2022–2023)
- among people aged 14 and over, males were more likely than females to currently use e-cigarettes (6.7% and 5.3%, respectively), but daily use was similar for both males and females (3.8% and 3.3%, respectively) (AIHW 2026, Table 2.5; Figure 2).

#### Why do people use vapes?

In 2025, the average age of initiation for e-cigarette use among people who currently use e-cigarettes was around 29 years, which has remained relatively stable over time (AIHW 2026, Table 2.26). This differed depending on the person’s tobacco smoking status when they first started using e-cigarettes, with the average age of initiation being:

For related content on e-cigarette use among specific population groups in this report, see Population groups.

- 24 years for those who had never smoked tobacco when they first tried an e-cigarette
- around 26 years for people who did not currently smoke tobacco when they first tried an e-cigarette
- around 32 years for people who currently smoked tobacco when they first tried an e-cigarette (AIHW 2026, Table 2.26).

- Updated Younger people are more likely to try e-cigarettes out of curiosity, while people in their 60s and over are more likely to try e-cigarettes to help them quit smoking. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Use of e-cigarettes is more common among younger age groups in Australia, and their reasons for using e-cigarettes are different to that of older people.

According to the 2025 NDSHS, among people who currently used e-cigarettes, the most common reasons for trying e-cigarettes were curiosity (37%) and enjoyment of using e-cigarettes (36%) (AIHW 2026, Table 2.28). However, this varied by age:

- Curiosity was the most common reason for e-cigarette use among people aged 14–17 (55%) and 18–24 (65%).
- Among people aged 30 and over, the most common reasons for e-cigarette use were to help quit or reduce smoking (ranging from 38% among those aged 40–49 to 49% among those aged 50–59) (AIHW 2026, Table 2.28; Figure 3).

#### Figure 3: Reasons for using e-cigarettes, people who currently use electronic cigarettes, by age, 2025

#### Does the use of vapes differ by geographic area?

Since 2016, current e-cigarette use has risen across all Australian remoteness areas, and socioeconomic areas (AIHW 2026, Table 2.7). Detailed information on e-cigarette use by geographic area in this report is available in Geographic areas.

### What do we know about people who use other nicotine products?

#### How many people use other nicotine products?

- New In 2025, 1.8% of the population aged 14 and over had used oral nicotine pouches in the previous 12 months. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

#### Does the use of other nicotine products differ by age and gender?

In the NDSHS, the questions relating to other nicotine product use changed between the 2022–2023 and 2025 surveys. In 2022–2023, participants were asked about their use of ‘snus’, while in 2025, this was replaced with the options ‘traditional snus (oral tobacco product)' and ‘oral nicotine pouches’.

Use of other and emerging nicotine products (such as snus and oral nicotine pouches) is relatively less common than use of e-cigarettes, but people who do use these products are also likely to use other forms of nicotine such as tobacco and/or e-cigarettes (AIHW 2026).

In the 2025 NDSHS, the recent use of traditional snus and oral nicotine pouches among the population aged 14 and over was 0.8% and 1.8%, respectively, while the proportion of people who reported recent use of snus in 2022–2023 was 0.4% (AIHW 2026, Table 2.40; Figure 4).

In 2025, almost half (49%) of people who used oral nicotine pouches said that they used e‑cigarettes at least monthly and over 1 in 4 (27%) used e-cigarettes daily. They were also more likely than the general population to smoke daily (12%) (AIHW 2026, tables 2.50 and 2.54).

- New In 2025, 1 in 12 people aged 18–24 had used oral nicotine products in the previous 12 months. Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Data from the 2025 NDSHS showed that the use of oral nicotine pouches and traditional (tobacco containing) snus was more common among males than females and among young people than older people, a similar pattern to e-cigarettes (AIHW 2026).

In the previous 12 months in 2025:

\* Estimate has a relative standard error of 25% to 50% and should be used with caution.

- among people aged 14 and over, males were more likely than females to have used oral nicotine pouches (2.9% and 0.7%, respectively) and traditional snus (1.4% and 0.3%, respectively)
- the use of oral nicotine pouches was most common among people aged 18–24 (8.4%) followed by people aged 25–29 (5.3%)
- the use of traditional snus was most common among people aged 18–24 (3.8%) followed by people aged 25–29 (*1.8%) (AIHW 2026, Table 2.40; Figure 4).

### What are the harms associated with the use of vapes and other nicotine products?

As e-cigarettes are relatively new, only becoming popular in the last 10–15 years, evidence for the longer-term health effects of their use is limited. However, over this relatively short time period, e-cigarette use has been associated with a range of health risks including nausea and vomiting, persistent coughing, nicotine dependence, respiratory problems and lung damage, and poisoning and seizures from excessive nicotine inhalation or ingestion of e-liquid (DHDA 2025). Illegal e-cigarettes can contain chemicals that are known to cause cancer, including formaldehyde and heavy metals like lead (DHDA 2025, Winnall et al. 2025).

Similarly, the long-term health effects of other and emerging nicotine products such as nicotine pouches remain unknown. However, use of any nicotine-containing products can contribute to dependence due to the addictive properties of nicotine. Use of nicotine pouches may contribute to harms including nausea, mouth lesions and dental damage, and potential cardiovascular effects (TGA 2026).

For the latest available information on harms related to e-cigarettes and other nicotine products, see Tobacco in Australia: Facts and issues.

### Where do I go for more information?

- Tobacco in Australia: Facts and issues
- Vaping and e-cigarette use in the NDSHS

## Alcohol, tobacco & other drugs in Australia, Alcohol

Source: https://www.aihw.gov.au/reports/alcohol/alcohol-tobacco-other-drugs-australia/contents/drug-types/alcohol

# Alcohol, tobacco & other drugs in Australia

## Alcohol

Alcohol
is an
Australia's health
topic
Explore related topic summaries

- Tobacco 07 Oct 2026
- Vapes and other nicotine products 07 Oct 2026
- Burden of disease 12 Dec 2024

				In this section

Introduction
What data sources are available?
What do we know about alcohol availability in Australia?
What do we know about people who consume alcohol?
What are the harms associated with alcohol use?
How many people receive treatment for alcohol use?
Where do I go for more information?

### Introduction

#### What is alcohol?

Alcohol is a depressant drug made from ethanol, typically consumed in the form of beer, wine, cider or spirits (ADF 2025). Alcohol concentration varies considerably with the type of drink. In Australia, beer contains 0.9–6% alcohol, wine contains 12–14%, fortified wines such as sherry and port contain around 18–20%, and spirits such as scotch, rum, bourbon and vodka contain 40–50% (NDARC 2017).

The consumption of alcohol is widespread within Australia and associated with many social and cultural activities. However, alcohol use is a leading risk factor for the burden of disease in Australia and is associated with a range of health and social harms including hospitalisation and death. The estimated social cost of alcohol use in Australia was $72.9 billion in 2020–21, projected to rise to $75.0 billion in 2022–23 (Gadsden et al. 2024, Table 1). The most significant projected costs in 2022–23 were related to premature mortality ($29.1 billion) and morbidity ($23.3 billion), and workplace-related costs ($4.5 billion) (Gadsden et al. 2024, Figure 2).

This page focuses on alcohol use, treatment and harms in Australia. The reporting uses data from a range of sources, mostly national administrative and survey data. For related content on policies and laws related to alcohol (including policies related to fetal alcohol spectrum disorder), see Policy context.

#### Key findings

- There were 217.1 million litres of pure alcohol available for consumption in Australia in 2023–24, down from 227.3 million litres in 2020–21
- Over two-thirds of people in Australia had consumed alcohol in the previous 12 months in 2022–2023, and 1 in 3 did so in ways that put their health at risk
- Alcohol has consistently accounted for a higher number of ambulance attendances, hospitalisations and deaths than any other drug over time
- More people receive specialist treatment for their use of alcohol than for other drugs including cannabis, methamphetamine and heroin

### What data sources are available?

#### Data sources for alcohol use, treatment and harms

- Alcohol and other drug treatment services in Australia
- Alcohol available for consumption in Australia
- Australian Burden of Disease Study
- Australia’s mothers and babies
- Causes of Death, Australia
- Household Income and Labour Dynamics in Australia (HILDA) survey
- Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme data collection
- National Ambulance Surveillance System
- National Drug Strategy Household Survey
- National Health Survey
- National Hospital Morbidity Database

### What do we know about alcohol availability in Australia?

There are a range of data sources that contain information about alcohol use, treatment and harms. These include self-report surveys of people who use alcohol, health administrative data (such as hospitalisations data), burden of disease analysis, and economic data (such as taxation data). Each data set uses a different methodology, and the language used to describe alcohol may also differ across sources.

For more information about each data source, see Technical notes.

- There were 9.8 litres of alcohol available for consumption per capita in 2023–24 Source: Alcohol available for consumption in Australia

In 2023, the mean annual spend on alcohol across Australian households was $1,770, or $34 per household per week on average. Household spending on alcohol has fluctuated over time but has declined since 2021 when the household average was $1,929 (Laß et al. 2025).

Long term data from the Alcohol available for consumption in Australia report show that the volume of alcohol available per capita has gradually decreased since the mid-1970s (AIHW 2025a). In 2023–24, there were 217.1 million litres of pure alcohol available for consumption in Australia, down from 227.3 million litres in 2020–21 (AIHW 2025a). The amount of alcohol available for consumption per capita has also fallen in recent years, from 10.9 litres in 2020–21 to 9.8 litres in 2023–24. Wine accounted for the largest proportion of pure alcohol available for consumption in 2023–24 (42%), followed by beer (32%) and spirits (23%) (AIHW 2025a; Figure 1).

#### Figure 1: Alcohol available for consumption in Australia by beverage type, 1944–45 to 2023–24

Of all pure alcohol in wine available for consumption in 2023–24, white and red wine made up a similar proportion (43% and 41% of total alcohol in wine, respectively). Full-strength beer was the most widely available form of beer in 2023–24, but the availability of mid-strength beer has risen and the availability of full- and low-strength beer has declined since 2008–09 (AIHW 2025a).

### What do we know about people who consume alcohol?

#### How many people consume alcohol and has it changed over time?

### Over 2 in 3

- people in Australia had consumed alcohol in the previous 12 months in 2022–2023 Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Most people in Australia aged 14 and older have consumed alcohol in their lifetime. The 2022–2023 National Drug Strategy Household Survey (NDSHS) found that of the population aged 14 and over:

- Over two-thirds (69%) had consumed alcohol in the previous 12 months.
- 14.9% had never consumed a full serve of alcohol.
- The proportion who consumed alcohol daily remained stable between 2019 (5.4%) and 2022–2023 (5.2%) (AIHW 2024b, tables 4.1 and 4.6; Figure 2).

#### Figure 2: Alcohol drinking status, people aged 14 and over, 2001 to 2022–2023

This line graph shows a steady decline in the proportion of people aged 14 and over who drink weekly or daily between 2001 and 2022–2023, with a slight uptick of weekly drinking between 2019 and 2022–2023. Over the same period, there was a moderate rise in the proportion of people who never drink, and a slight rise in the proportion of people who drink less than monthly. The proportion of people are ex-drinkers remained relatively stable between 2001 and 2022–2023. Weekly drinking was consistently the category that contained the highest proportion of people.

(a) Consumed at least a full serve of alcohol, but has not had an alcoholic drink in the previous 12 months.

#### How many people consume alcohol at risky levels, and does this differ by age and gender?

#### What is risky alcohol consumption?

In the 2022–2023 NDSHS, “drinking at risky levels” or “risky drinking” is defined according to Guideline 1 of the Australian guidelines to reduce health risks from drinking alcohol (NHMRC 2020). In the NDSHS, a person doing either or both of the following is classified as having consumed alcohol in ways that increased their risk of harm:

The full guidelines state:

1. Having more than 10 standard drinks per week on average in the previous 12 months.
2. Having more than 4 standard drinks in a single day at least once a month over the previous 12 months.

- Guideline 1: To reduce the risk of harm from alcohol-related disease or injury, healthy men and women should drink no more than 10 standard drinks a week and no more than 4 standard drinks on any one day.
- Guideline 2: To reduce the risk of injury and other harms to health, children and people under 18 years of age should not drink alcohol.
- Guideline 3: To prevent harm from alcohol to their unborn child, women who are pregnant or planning a pregnancy should not drink alcohol. For women who are breastfeeding, not drinking alcohol is safest for their baby (NHMRC 2020).

### Around 1 in 3

- people aged 14 and over consumed alcohol at risky levels in 2022–2023 Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

The National Health and Medical Research Council (NHMRC) has released risk guidelines to reduce the risk of alcohol-related harm. The NDSHS found around 1 in 3 people (31% or 6.6 million people) aged 14 and over consumed alcohol at risky levels (per the adult risk guidelines) in 2022–2023. This is a similar level to 2019, when 32% of the population (around 6.7 million people) reported drinking at risky levels (AIHW 2024b, tables 4.25 and 4.26; Figure 3).

#### Figure 3: Alcohol consumption and risk, people aged 14 and over, by age, 2001 to 2022–2023

#### How many women consume alcohol during pregnancy?

The 2022–2023 NDSHS found that:

Data from the 2022 National Health Survey (NHS) were collected against the 2020 Australian guidelines to reduce health risks from drinking alcohol. Estimates using self-reported data showed that in 2022:

- Males continued to be more likely than females to exceed adult risk guidelines for alcohol (39% of males aged 14 and over and 23% of females aged 14 and over).
- Fewer younger people (those aged 14-17) exceeded adult risk guidelines (5.5% in 2022–2023, compared with 9.5% in 2019) (AIHW 2024b, Table 4.28).

- More than 1 in 4 (26.8%) Australians aged 18 and over exceeded Guideline 1 of the Australian Alcohol Guidelines. This includes people who consumed more than 10 drinks in the last week and/or consumed 5 or more drinks in any day at least monthly in the last 12 months.
- Men were more likely than women to exceed the guideline (35.8% compared with 18.1%).
- People born in Australia were more than twice as likely as those born overseas to exceed the guideline (33.0% compared with 16.0%) (ABS 2023).

- 28% Over 1 in 4 pregnant women consumed alcohol at some point during their pregnancy in 2022–2023, most often before they knew they were pregnant Source: National Drug Strategy Household Survey

Alcohol consumption during pregnancy can lead to poorer perinatal outcomes including low birthweight, pre-term birth and fetal alcohol spectrum disorder (FASD). People with FASD are affected for life and can experience challenges with learning, communication, memory and regulating behaviour and emotions (NOFASD 2026).

An estimated 1 in 28 or 3.6% (95% confidence interval 2.9%, 4.4%) people in Australia have FASD, based on the best available data on prenatal alcohol exposure from a recent systematic review with meta-analysis (Tsang et al. 2025). This is similar to other high-income countries such as Canada and the United States (Tsang et al. 2025).

The NHMRC advises that women who are planning a pregnancy, or are pregnant, should not drink alcohol. Support to address alcohol consumption is available through antenatal clinics (AIHW 2025b).

Data on maternal consumption of alcohol during pregnancy were available in the National Perinatal Data Collection for 6 of 8 jurisdictions in 2023 (excluding New South Wales and the Australian Capital Territory). In 2023, most women who gave birth reported they did not consume alcohol during their pregnancy: 2.4% of women reported consuming alcohol in the first 20 weeks of pregnancy, decreasing to 0.5% of women after 20 weeks of pregnancy (AIHW 2025b). In 2023, reported consumption of alcohol in the first 20 weeks of pregnancy was more common among women who:

However, women from these population groups showed a decrease in reported alcohol consumption after 20 weeks of pregnancy to:

Some women may consume alcohol before they know they are pregnant and stop once they find out they are pregnant. Data from the 2022–2023 NDSHS showed that:

- lived in Remote (4.0%) or Very remote (7.6%) areas
- were aged under 20 (5.5%) (AIHW 2025b).

- 1.5% in Remote areas and 3.6% in Very remote areas
- 1.3% of women aged under 20 (AIHW 2025b).

- Over 1 in 4 (28%) women who had been pregnant in the past 12 months consumed alcohol at some stage during their pregnancy, similar to 29% in 2019.
- Of pregnant women who reported a period of time they were unaware of their pregnancy, 64% had consumed alcohol before knowing they were pregnant.
- After they became aware of their pregnancy, women were much less likely to consume alcohol (AIHW 2024b, Table 10.21).

#### Does alcohol use differ by geographic area?

Since 2010, the proportion of people aged 14 and over consuming alcohol at risky levels has declined across all states and territories, socioeconomic areas and remoteness areas (AIHW 2024b).

Detailed information on alcohol and other drug use by geographic areas, including state and territory data, is available in Geographic areas.

For related content on alcohol use among specific population groups in this report, see Population groups.

### What are the harms associated with alcohol use?

Alcohol is absorbed rapidly in the bloodstream and affects the brain within about 5 minutes, though this may vary from person to person depending on body mass and general state of health (NDARC 2017). Short-term effects of alcohol such as a sense of relaxation and reduced inhibitions, may add to the appeal of its consumption. However, when consumed in excess, alcohol can also produce unpleasant effects such as nausea and vomiting and may influence people to engage in harmful behaviour (Table 1).

Source: NDARC (2017).

**Table 1: Effects of alcohol consumption**

| Short-term effects | Long-term effects |
|---|---|
| Reduced inhibitionsA sense of relaxationLoss of alertness or coordination, and slower reaction timesImpaired memory and judgementNausea, shakiness, and vomitingBlurred or double visionDisturbed sleep patternsDisturbed sexual functioning | Oral, throat, and breast cancersLiver cirrhosisBrain damage and dementiaSome forms of heart disease and stroke |

#### How does alcohol use contribute to the burden of disease and injury?

- Alcohol use was responsible for 4.1% of the total burden of disease and injury in Australia in 2024 Source: Australian Burden of Disease Study

The Australian Burden of Disease Study 2024 found that alcohol use was the sixth highest risk factor contributing to the burden of disease in Australia and was responsible for 4.1% of the total burden of disease and injury (Table ABDS1). The age-standardised rate of total attributable burden due to alcohol use was 8.3 Disability-Adjusted Life Years (DALY) per 1,000 population in 2024, a 13.5% decline from 9.6 DALY per 1,000 population in 2003.

Alcohol use contributed to a number of diseases and injuries including:

For related content on the burden of disease in this report, see Burden of disease and injuries related to alcohol and other drugs.

- 100% of the burden due to alcohol use disorders
- 39% of the burden due to liver cancer
- 26% of the burden due to road traffic injuries involving motor vehicle occupants
- 20% of the burden due to chronic liver disease
- 13% of the burden due to suicide and self-inflicted injuries
- 11% of the burden due to breast cancer (AIHW 2024a, Table S6).

#### Alcohol-related injuries

The definitions of alcohol-related conditions in the following injury statistics are different to those reported elsewhere. For more information, see Alcohol-related injury: hospitalisations and deaths, 2019–20.

In 2019–20, alcohol-related injuries accounted for 5.7% of all injury hospitalisations and 14% of all injury deaths (AIHW 2023). For more information, see Alcohol-related injury: hospitalisations and deaths, 2019–20.

The 2022–2023 NDSHS reported those who consumed alcohol at risky levels were:

For related content on injuries in this report, see Burden of disease and injuries related to alcohol and other drugs.

- 14 times as likely to experience an injury requiring medical attention while under the influence of alcohol (4.2% compared with *0.3%) as those who did not drink at risky levels
- 10.5 times as likely to have experienced an injury requiring admission to hospital while under the influence of alcohol (2.1% compared with *0.2%) (*Estimate has a relative standard error between 25% and 50% and should be used with caution) (AIHW 2024b, Table 4.52).

#### Alcohol-related ambulance attendances

###

- 529 575 The rate of alcohol intoxication-related ambulance attendances increased from 529 to 575 per 100,000 population between 2022 and 2023 Source: National Ambulance Surveillance System

#### Alcohol-related hospitalisations

Data on alcohol and other drug-related ambulance attendances are sourced from the National Ambulance Surveillance System (NASS) and are currently available for six of the eight Australian states and territories (excluding Western Australia and South Australia).

Alcohol intoxication accounts for the highest number and rate of alcohol and other drug-related ambulance attendances. There were over 99,200 ambulance attendances for alcohol intoxication among people aged 15 and over in 2024, or 540 per 100,000 population (Table NASS3).

Among these attendances:

Between 2021 and 2023, the number and rate of alcohol intoxication-related ambulance attendances overall increased from around 97,300 (565 per 100,000 population) to around 103,400 (575 per 100,000 population). The number and rate of attendances overall decreased between 2023 and 2024, largely due to a decrease in attendances in Victoria (from around 27,900 attendances (498 per 100,000 population) to around 17,300 (302 per 100,000 population)). This is explained by industrial action by paramedics in Victoria between March and September 2024, which resulted in fewer ambulance attendances being captured over that period. 2024 data should be interpreted with caution (Table NASS3).

For related content on ambulance attendances in this report, see Alcohol and other drug-related ambulance attendances.

- almost 3 in 5 (59%) were for males
- the highest rate of attendances was for people aged 45–54 (740 per 100,000 population) (Table NASS3).

- Alcohol accounted for almost 3 in 5 of all drug-related hospitalisations in 2023–24 Source: National Hospital Morbidity Database

#### Alcohol-induced deaths

Alcohol has continued to account for a higher rate of hospitalisations than any other drug over the past decade, though alcohol-related hospitalisations have remained stable in recent years (see Figure 1). Alcohol accounted for almost 3 in 5 drug-related hospitalisations in 2023–24 (58% or 84,318 hospitalisations), a rate of 312.8 hospitalisations per 100,000 people (tables NHMD2 and NHMD3) (see Figure 1).

Among alcohol-related hospitalisations in 2023–24:

- Over half (52% or 44,234 alcohol-related hospitalisations) involved an overnight stay, while the remainder ended with a same-day discharge.
- Almost 3 in 5 (59% or 49,354) were for males.
- 3 in 5 (60% or 50,306) were for people aged 45 years and over (tables NHMD1–NHMD3).

- 9 in 10 alcohol-induced deaths in 2024 were related to chronic conditions like liver cirrhosis Source: Causes of Death, Australia

**Release of preliminary deaths data for 2024**

Preliminary causes of death data for deaths registered in 2024 were made available on the ABS website in mid-November 2025, including updated data tables for both alcohol- and drug-induced deaths. Estimates for 2022, 2023 and 2024 are expected to rise with standard revision processes.

Preliminary estimates from Causes of Death, Australia indicate there were 1,765 alcohol-induced deaths in 2024, higher than for any other drug. This represents an age-standardised rate of 5.9 deaths per 100,000 people (ABS 2025, Table 13.11). The number and rate of alcohol-induced deaths has risen over the past decade, from 1,362 deaths in 2015 (5.2 per 100,000 people) (ABS 2025, Table 13.11).

Among all alcohol-induced deaths in 2024:

AIHW analysis of the National Mortality Database showed that of the 1,765 alcohol-induced deaths registered in 2024:

For related content on deaths in this report, see Deaths involving alcohol and other drugs.

- 9 in 10 (90%, or 1,585) deaths were related to chronic conditions such as liver cirrhosis, while the remainder were due to acute conditions such as alcohol poisoning (10% or 180 deaths).
- The death rate was higher for males than females (8.9 compared with 3.0 deaths per 100,000 population, respectively).
- The median age at death was 58.4 years (ABS 2025, tables 13.11, 13.15 and 13.16).

- The highest age-specific rates were for older people – 16 per 100,000 population for those aged 55–64 and 13 for those aged 45–54 (Table NMD6).
- The majority (74% or 1,311 deaths) were recorded for males (Table NMD5).
- Almost half (47% or 837 deaths) recorded mental and behavioural disorders due to psychoactive substance use as an associated cause of death (that is, a factor that contributed to the death but was not the underlying cause of death) (Table NMD8).

### How many people receive treatment for alcohol use?

#### Alcohol cessation medicines

Data from Australia’s Pharmaceutical Benefits Scheme (PBS) provide information about prescription medicines that are used to help people stop or reduce their alcohol consumption (alcohol cessation medicines), including the number of prescriptions dispensed and the number of patients dispensed a script. Data from the PBS show that rates of dispensing for alcohol cessation medicines have risen between 2012–13 and 2024–25 (tables PBS78 and PBS80).

#### Specialist treatment for alcohol use

- Updated Alcohol was the principal drug of concern in more than 2 in 5 (41%, around 87,600) specialist treatment episodes in 2024–25, up from around 1 in 3 (32%, around 63,200) in 2015–16 Source: Alcohol and other drug treatment services in Australia report

### Where do I go for more information?

Data from the Alcohol and other drug treatment services in Australia annual report show that alcohol continues to be the most common principal drug of concern (AIHW 2026). Alcohol was the principal drug of concern in more than 2 in 5 (41%, around 87,600) treatment episodes provided to people for their own drug use in 2024–25, stable from 42% (around 91,400 episodes) in 2023–24 and up from 32% (around 63,200) in 2015–16 (AIHW 2026).

Of the approximately 46,800 clients who received treatment for alcohol as their principal drug of concern in 2024–25:

Over 3 in 5 (65% or 56,300 episodes) treatment episodes provided to clients for their own use of alcohol in 2024–25 were for people who had previously received AOD treatment since 2013–14 (AIHW 2026, Table SCR.28a).

For detailed information on alcohol cessation medicines and alcohol and other drug treatment in this report, see:

- over 3 in 5 (62%) were male
- 1 in 2 were aged either 30–39 (24% of clients) or 40–49 (26%)
- 1 in 6 (16%) were First Nations people (AIHW 2026, tables SC.9–SC.11).

- Alcohol and other drug treatment services
- Availability of smoking and alcohol cessation medicines in Australia
